Hence (115) passes to the infinite weak-depth limit termwise for every fixed n.
Proof. For one conditional integration, differentiation of the normalized logarithm is the joint-cumulant
identity. The set-partition formula for a derivative of a composition is precisely the Banach-valued Faà di
Bruno formula: every block of the partition is one incoming connected component and the outer cumulant
joins those blocks at the current scale. Fixing the least-label order makes this representation unique.
Induction over the finite sequence of exact conditional integrations proves (115); the terminal matching
logarithm is the final induction step. The weak rooted margin pays every nonroot weak vertex by σ < 1,
while the finite macrobridge and matching KP expansion pay the finitely many terminal vertices. The
one/two-root bounds control a component while its source leaves remain external. These majorants are
summable independently of m, so dominated convergence gives the last assertion.

    For a tree carrying two distinguished source buffers, let v(A, B) be their unique lowest common ancestor.
If v(A, B) belongs to the terminal matching logarithm, call the history a matching history. Otherwise its
scale label j(v) is the unique weak coarsest-connection scale. Notice that this is a property of the exact
recursively generated tree, not a choice made after absolute values or bounds have been applied.
Corollary 8.7 (No omission and no double counting). The connected two-buffer part of D2 Wm (0) is
the disjoint sum of the matching histories and the weak histories indexed by their unique label j ≥ jw .
Contracting all strict descendants of v(A, B) produces a one/two-root decorated vertex whose norm is
bounded uniformly by Theorem 7.2 and Corollary 7.4. The decomposition remains exact after regular and
irregular sectors are recombined. Every connected tree has exactly one lowest common ancestor for the two
source leaves, and the recursive least-label convention gives it exactly one representation in Lemma 8.6.
Descendants have strictly finer labels or belong to the same finite conditional vertex and are summed by
its connected cumulant. Exact regular/irregular recombination precedes the conditional logarithm at every
scale, so the forest formula expands the full measure rather than a conditioned all-good measure.

Unique-coarsest-scale allocation Differentiate the exact composition of normalized conditional
logarithms with respect to source parameters. Every connected term has a finite collection of local objects
carrying scale labels. Contract every maximal connected collection of finer-scale objects into a decorated
root and declare the largest physical scale remaining to be the coarsest scale of the history. Ties are
broken lexicographically.
Lemma 8.8 (Canonical multiscale allocation). Every connected two-buffer source history is assigned
exactly once either to the matching connector or to one weak scale j ≥ jw . Finer decorations attached to
its coarsest skeleton have a uniformly summable one/two-root norm. Put

Proof. By Corollary 8.7, the lowest common ancestor of the two source buffers assigns every connected

they are contracted into the exact local atom described in the statement.

degraded by increasing the ultraviolet depth.
   For bounded observables, the covariance and every short-distance connected local atom are bounded
by their sup norms, so enlarging A0 extends the bound to all separations. Lemma 8.1 and integrability of
the physical exponential kernel give (118). For a normalized local source, the short-distance cluster is
not estimated pointwise. It is exactly the local source coefficient renormalized by the finite triangular
counterterm matrix; the finite triangular source theorem printed in Part V bounds it in a finite Schwartz
seminorm. Once two source supports are separated, the lowest-common-ancestor edge lies outside both
local clusters and the same connector exponent applies. Thus the theorem derives a cutoff-independent
physical rate without suppressing or hiding contact terms.

Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.
If two groups of source supports are separated by physical distance d, their connected full moments are
bounded by Ce−md with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3,
together with the finite compact matching expansion, give a common analytic source ball. Theorem 8.11
permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are
chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra
and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.
In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation;
factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.

Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered,
permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for
the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized
local source family. The distributions are projectively compatible and satisfy uniform physical exponential
clustering.
Proof. The Schwartz estimate gives equicontinuity on each finite tensor power. Diagonal extraction over
a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes
as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact
label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12
gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row.

8.2    Ward identities and Euclidean covariance
Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie
algebra g:
(I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space;
(I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four;
(I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection-
     even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density;
(I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color
     tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its
      presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block.
The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every
fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension.
Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite-
dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity
then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature
scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra
finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric
tensor exists only for type Ar , and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension
six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions
occur.

 (b) If either buffer carries a fixed finite renormalized local source, displacement mark, or rotation mark,

     by Cs,A,B e−md . At shorter distance it is controlled in the local Schwartz/source seminorm supplied
     by the triangular finite-jet renormalization; no cutoff-uniform pointwise estimate on the diagonal is
     claimed.
The exponent m is common to both classes and to every fixed finite source family.
Proof. Combine Lemma 8.5 with the exact forest allocation and the weak scale sum. For d ≥ dloc choose
dloc so the variable x = mw (d − CR )+ in Lemma 8.8 is at least one. Weak histories then decay with
rate at least mw /2, while matching histories decay with rate at least 12 min{mtr , msp }. Hence one may
take the common exponent m = m∗ from (111); Lemma 8.2 shows explicitly that this exponent is not
degraded by increasing the ultraviolet depth.
   For bounded observables, the covariance and every short-distance connected local atom are bounded
by their sup norms, so enlarging A0 extends the bound to all separations. Lemma 8.1 and integrability of
the physical exponential kernel give (118). For a normalized local source, the short-distance cluster is
not estimated pointwise. It is exactly the local source coefficient renormalized by the finite triangular
counterterm matrix; the finite triangular source theorem printed in Part V bounds it in a finite Schwartz
seminorm. Once two source supports are separated, the lowest-common-ancestor edge lies outside both
local clusters and the same connector exponent applies. Thus the theorem derives a cutoff-independent
physical rate without suppressing or hiding contact terms.

Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.
If two groups of source supports are separated by physical distance d, their connected full moments are
bounded by Ce−md with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3,
together with the finite compact matching expansion, give a common analytic source ball. Theorem 8.11
permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are
chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra
and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.
In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation;
factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.

Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered,

gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row.

8.2    Ward identities and Euclidean covariance
Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie
algebra g:
(I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space;
(I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four;
(I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection-
     even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density;
(I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color
     tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its
      presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block.
The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every
fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension.
Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite-
dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity
then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature
scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra
finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric
tensor exists only for type Ar , and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension
six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions
occur.

generated scale-labelled connected trees described above. Each
analytic coefficient of each exact conditional logarithm occurs exactly once. After absolute values are
taken, the sum is dominated uniformly in m by the rooted weak rows and the finite matching expansion.
Hence (115) passes to the infinite weak-depth limit termwise for every fixed n.
Proof. For one conditional integration, differentiation of the normalized logarithm is the joint-cumulant
identity. The set-partition formula for a derivative of a composition is precisely the Banach-valued Faà di
Bruno formula: every block of the partition is one incoming connected component and the outer cumulant
joins those blocks at the current scale. Fixing the least-label order makes this representation unique.
Induction over the finite sequence of exact conditional integrations proves (115); the terminal matching
logarithm is the final induction step. The weak rooted margin pays every nonroot weak vertex by σ < 1,
while the finite macrobridge and matching KP expansion pay the finitely many terminal vertices. The
one/two-root bounds control a component while its source leaves remain external. These majorants are
summable independently of m, so dominated convergence gives the last assertion.

    For a tree carrying two distinguished source buffers, let v(A, B) be their unique lowest common ancestor.
If v(A, B) belongs to the terminal matching logarithm, call the history a matching history. Otherwise its
scale label j(v) is the unique weak coarsest-connection scale. Notice that this is a property of the exact
recursively generated tree, not a choice made after absolute values or bounds have been applied.
Corollary 8.7 (No omission and no double counting). The connected two-buffer part of D2 Wm (0) is
the disjoint sum of the matching histories and the weak histories indexed by their unique label j ≥ jw .
Contracting all strict descendants of v(A, B) produces a one/two-root decorated vertex whose norm is
bounded uniformly by Theorem 7.2 and Corollary 7.4. The decomposition remains exact after regular and
irregular sectors are recombined. Every connected tree has exactly one lowest common ancestor for the two
source leaves, and the recursive least-label convention gives it exactly one representation in Lemma 8.6.
Descendants have strictly finer labels or belong to the same finite conditional vertex and are summed by
its connected cumulant. Exact regular/irregular recombination precedes the conditional logarithm at every
scale, so the forest formula expands the full measure rather than a conditioned all-good measure.

Unique-coarsest-scale allocation Differentiate the exact composition of normalized conditional
logarithms with respect to source parameters. Every connected term has a finite collection of local objects
carrying scale labels. Contract every maximal connected collection of finer-scale objects into a decorated
root and declare the largest physical scale remaining to be the coarsest scale of the history. Ties are
broken lexicographically.
Lemma 8.8 (Canonical multiscale allocation). Every connected two-buffer source history is assigned
exactly once either to the matching connector or to one weak scale j ≥ jw . Finer decorations attached to
its coarsest skeleton have a uniformly summable one/two-root norm. Put

For d < dw the complete history is retained in one fixed enlarged local cluster: it is uniformly bounded for
bounded observables and controlled in the local source topology for renormalized fields.
Proof. By Corollary 8.7, the lowest common ancestor of the two source buffers assigns every connected
tree to exactly one terminal or weak-scale class. This is the first scale in the exact recursive conditional-
logarithm formula at which the two source components are joined. The least-label convention removes
automorphism multiplicities before estimates are applied. The fixed root algebra sums every finer
decoration independently of the number of activePnonroot supports. Since ℓj = 2−(j−jw ) ℓjw , put
x = mw (d − CR )+ . The scale sum is bounded by C r≥0 exp(−2r x). The condition d ≥ dw gives x ≥ 1,
and then this is at most C1 exp(−x), proving (116). If d < dw the two buffers lie in one fixed enlarged
physical neighborhood. Their ultraviolet contact subtrees are not summed as separate long connectors;
they are contracted into the exact local atom described in the statement.

resolution, microscopic cutoff, volume, and boundary conditions, with the following properties.
 (a) For two centered bounded positive-scale observables supported in BR (z) and BR (z ′ ), the complete

The exponent m is common to both classes and to every fixed finite source family.
Proof. Combine Lemma 8.5 with the exact forest allocation and the weak scale sum. For d ≥ dloc choose
dloc so the variable x = mw (d − CR )+ in Lemma 8.8 is at least one. Weak histories then decay with
rate at least mw /2, while matching histories decay with rate at least 12 min{mtr , msp }. Hence one may
take the common exponent m = m∗ from (111); Lemma 8.2 shows explicitly that this exponent is not
degraded by increasing the ultraviolet depth.
   For bounded observables, the covariance and every short-distance connected local atom are bounded
by their sup norms, so enlarging A0 extends the bound to all separations. Lemma 8.1 and integrability of
the physical exponential kernel give (118). For a normalized local source, the short-distance cluster is
not estimated pointwise. It is exactly the local source coefficient renormalized by the finite triangular
counterterm matrix; the finite triangular source theorem printed in Part V bounds it in a finite Schwartz
seminorm. Once two source supports are separated, the lowest-common-ancestor edge lies outside both
local clusters and the same connector exponent applies. Thus the theorem derives a cutoff-independent
physical rate without suppressing or hiding contact terms.

Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.
If two groups of source supports are separated by physical distance d, their connected full moments are
bounded by Ce−md with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3,
together with the finite compact matching expansion, give a common analytic source ball. Theorem 8.11
permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are
chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra
and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.
In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation;
factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.

proves the claim.
Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered,
permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for
the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized
local source family. The distributions are projectively compatible and satisfy uniform physical exponential
clustering.
Proof. The Schwartz estimate gives equicontinuity on each finite tensor power. Diagonal extraction over
a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes
as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact
label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12
gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row.

8.2    Ward identities and Euclidean covariance
Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie
algebra g:
(I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space;
(I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four;
(I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection-
     even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density;
(I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color
     tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its
      presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block.
The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every
fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension.
Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite-
dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity
then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature
scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra
finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric
tensor exists only for type Ar , and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension
six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions
occur.

8.2.3 Dimension-raised defect after the finite counterterm

times a finite dimension-six jet. The important point is that this statement is applied only after the
complete filtered low-jet matrix has been normalized.

 (b) If either buffer carries a fixed finite renormalized local source, displacement mark, or rotation mark,

The exponent m is common to both classes and to every fixed finite source family.
Proof. Combine Lemma 8.5 with the exact forest allocation and the weak scale sum. For d ≥ dloc choose
dloc so the variable x = mw (d − CR )+ in Lemma 8.8 is at least one. Weak histories then decay with
rate at least mw /2, while matching histories decay with rate at least 12 min{mtr , msp }. Hence one may
take the common exponent m = m∗ from (111); Lemma 8.2 shows explicitly that this exponent is not
degraded by increasing the ultraviolet depth.
   For bounded observables, the covariance and every short-distance connected local atom are bounded
by their sup norms, so enlarging A0 extends the bound to all separations. Lemma 8.1 and integrability of
the physical exponential kernel give (118). For a normalized local source, the short-distance cluster is
not estimated pointwise. It is exactly the local source coefficient renormalized by the finite triangular
counterterm matrix; the finite triangular source theorem printed in Part V bounds it in a finite Schwartz
seminorm. Once two source supports are separated, the lowest-common-ancestor edge lies outside both
local clusters and the same connector exponent applies. Thus the theorem derives a cutoff-independent
physical rate without suppressing or hiding contact terms.

Lemma 8.10 (Labelled-tree extraction). Every connected source-labelled history admits a deterministic
allocation to a spanning tree on {1, . . . , n}. An edge joining bounded positive-scale roots is bounded by
Theorem 8.9(a). For renormalized local sources, every contact subtree is first contracted into one local
renormalized vertex; an edge joining two disjoint vertices is then bounded by Theorem 8.9(b). Only the
two endpoint clusters of one edge are retained as roots, and all previously exposed variables are exterior
data.



                                                          76
Proof. Start with the least label and expose the connected history until it first reaches the least unseen
label. Draw that edge, absorb the exposed component into the parent cluster, and repeat. Every new
label is attached once. When an edge is estimated, Theorem 6.44 treats the two endpoint clusters as the
only distinguished roots and is uniform in all already exposed exterior variables.

for one finite Schwartz seminorm pN depending on the source types. If the source labels split into two

Proof. Differentiate the connected source logarithm and first collect each maximal contact subtree into
one local root. Apply Lemma 8.10; Theorem 6.44 shows that no matching-stage derivative of order
n is required, because each tree edge sees only its two endpoint clusters as distinguished roots. For
bounded roots, every edge has the pointwise connector (117); excess attachments are summed by the
unused strict KP margin, and the deterministic allocation costs at most the displayed factorial. For

remaining edges join disjoint clusters and carry the separated-support exponential from Theorem 8.9(b).
The local vertices are bounded by the triangular source-renormalization seminorms. Repeated Schur
summation of the remaining edges, with one Schwartz L1 norm at the tree root, gives (120) and the
separated-group exponential.
Corollary 8.12 (Uniform source analyticity and exponential clustering). On a fixed neighborhood of the
origin, every finite source functional is analytic uniformly in resolution and volume. Thermodynamic
and zero-spacing limits of all fixed finite source derivatives exist along one common diagonal subsequence.
If two groups of source supports are separated by physical distance d, their connected full moments are
bounded by Ce−md with the same m > 0 as in Theorem 8.9. The local source incidence and Lemma 8.3,
together with the finite compact matching expansion, give a common analytic source ball. Theorem 8.11
permits termwise differentiation and a countable diagonal extraction. The finite-volume approximants are
chosen on a cofinal schedule from Corollary 6.2. The extraction is made once for the countable algebra
and a fixed countable set of dyadic physical source scales, and the resulting subsequence is relabelled as
the directed sequence appearing in Theorem 1.1; no uniqueness or schedule-independence claim is needed.
In every connected partition joining two separated groups, at least one tree edge crosses the separation;
factor its exponential and sum all remaining edges with (118). Full moments follow from the set-partition
formula.

Lemma 8.14 (Reflection positivity inheritance under exact recombination). Every limiting positive-time
quadratic form used in the common Schwinger system is the limit of a reflection-positive quadratic form
under the original Wilson measure. Exact normal-chart, coarea, compact-pivot, and source transfor-

Proof. At finite cutoff and aligned reflection volume, let Fi be any finite family of constructed positive-time

The equality is unchanged by the exact coordinate transformations because all Jacobian and normalization

the common subsequence, so the closed positive-semidefinite cone contains the limiting matrix. This
proves the claim.
Theorem 8.15 (Common positive-scale Schwinger system). The common subsequence defines tempered,
permutation-symmetric, reflection-positive, continuously translation-covariant Schwinger distributions for
the countable algebra generated by the bounded positive-scale observables and every fixed finite normalized
local source family. The distributions are projectively compatible and satisfy uniform physical exponential
clustering.
Proof. The Schwartz estimate gives equicontinuity on each finite tensor power. Diagonal extraction over
a countable dense test/source family gives the common projective system. Permutation symmetry passes
as an algebraic identity, while Lemma 8.14 gives the complete reflection-positivity mechanism after exact
label recombination. Riemann- sum translation errors vanish by the physical Schur bound, Corollary 8.12
gives clustering, and Lemma 8.13 gives the OS linear-growth row.

8.2    Ward identities and Euclidean covariance
Theorem 8.16 (Fixed-group invariant-jet admission through dimension six). For a compact simple Lie
algebra g:
(I1) the invariant symmetric bilinear forms form a one-dimensional space;
(I2) there is no reflection-compatible gauge-invariant local vector of engineering dimension at most four;
(I3) modulo total differences, equations of motion, and the reflection-odd topological density, the reflection-
     even scalar dimension-four block is generated by the Yang–Mills density;
(I4) the gauge-invariant jet quotient through dimension six is finite. A primitive cubic symmetric color
     tensor occurs precisely for Lie type Ar with r ≥ 2 and is retained in the dimension-six block; its
      presence does not enlarge the dimension-four marginal block.
The filtered finite-matrix transport and associated-graded contraction used below therefore apply for every
fixed G after enlarging only the finite jet dimension.
Proof. Simplicity gives the uniqueness of the invariant bilinear form. Semisimplicity makes every finite-
dimensional representation traceless on g. Lorentz index counting, gauge covariance, and reflection parity
then exclude a single free vector index through dimension four and leave only the quadratic curvature
scalar in the even dimension-four sector. Chevalley’s theorem makes the invariant polynomial algebra
finitely generated; the degrees are the exponents plus one. Through degree three a primitive symmetric
tensor exists only for type Ar , and it first multiplies three curvatures, hence has engineering dimension
six. At every fixed engineering cutoff only finitely many covariant derivatives and invariant contractions
occur.

8.2.3 Dimension-raised defect after the finite counterterm

times a finite dimension-six jet. The important point is that this statement is applied only after the
complete filtered low-jet matrix has been normalized.

Lemma 8.27 (Exact profile division). At every fixed field degree, the constant-profile vector kernel of the

The constants are independent of cutoff, finite volume, weak depth, and fine-cell count. Moreover, on the

Proof. The source is a globally smooth fixed-range function of finitely many links. On the regular weak

derivatives enter the already established irregular activity row. Lemma 8.18 removes the vacuum Haar
trace before any support norm, and Lemma 8.19 removes the field-linear color trace. Taylor-expand the
smooth profile on each link neighborhood. Lemma 8.27 moves the potentially dangerous constant-profile
low jet onto ∇v by a signed algebraic identity. Theorem 8.26 identifies the dimension-four quotient
with the traceless stress and scalar coupling-conjugate channels; the antisymmetric part is the already
controlled rotation/total-difference row. Every other regular term contains a second profile derivative,
two additional lattice derivatives, an equation-of-motion contact, or a nonreference root. After canonical
rescaling these give Cℓj p2 (v).
    There is no source-chart singularity on the irregular complement: Ωsin and all of its group derivatives
are bounded globally. Use the same regular/irregular partition and Wilson-deficit estimate as the all-stage
local activity theorem. The exact profile division and canonical dimension separation are performed
before absolute root summation, so the explicit lattice-scale factor produces either a discrete derivative
of the profile or a dimension-six remainder. The remaining fixed local derivative losses are absorbed by
ϵirr (uj ). Root at the first source link, preserve signs through the vacuum and Laurent identities, and
then invoke Theorem 7.3 and Corollary 7.4. A second source derivative acts on finitely many local profile
factors and is controlled by the same common marked-C 3 row. Exact conditional pushforward and the
joint matching connector preserve these bounds with a fixed factor. Finally Proposition 8.21 gives the
O(a2 ) replacement error on bounded geometric representatives.

Theorem 8.33 (Projective stress Schwinger distribution). Along the common directed subsequence, the
limit
                           W(f ; A1 , . . . , An ) = lim Wj (f ; A1 , . . . , An )            (159)
                                                         j→∞

exists simultaneously for the countable rational Schwartz/source core and extends uniquely by continuity.
It is:
(S1) linear and Schwartz tempered in f ;
(S2) multilinear and continuous in bounded exterior cylinders;
(S3) local relative to the common gauge-invariant source system;
(S4) continuously translation covariant and signed-hypercubic covariant;
(S5) independent of the compatible microscopic representative.
Proof. Apply (154) between consecutive resolutions. The exact finite cocycle moves the retained leading
jet to the next scale, while the reported high-jet route is part of Rj ; Lemma 8.30 bounds the total
difference by the summable sequence (156). Thus every matrix element is Cauchy. The uniform tree bound
gives a finite Schwartz seminorm and permits diagonal extraction on the countable core. Translation
continuity, locality, clustering, and representative independence follow from the uniform rooted source-tree
bound and the summable recurrence above. The tensor source is signed-hypercubic at finite cutoff. Its
complete filtered low-jet block is normalized by Theorem 8.24; the remaining antisymmetric regulator
response is two engineering dimensions higher after one or two geometric marks. The angular Ward
theorem below then integrates the resulting infinitesimal action to O(4) covariance.

Proposition 8.36 (Noncollapse and canonical normalization). The stress Schwinger distribution is
nonzero. Its normalization is fixed uniquely by the unit contact coefficient in (160); no independent finite
factor is available.
Proof. The reflected curvature source of Theorem 10.5 (proved independently in Section 10) defines
a nonzero centered local vector. Common physical clustering gives its time-translation correlation a
finite positive spectral measure supported away from zero; hence that correlation is not constant and its
distributional translation derivative is nonzero for suitable separated test profiles. Choose v supported
near one insertion. The right side of (160) is then nonzero, so the tensor insertion on the left cannot
vanish. Multiplying the tensor by a finite constant would multiply the left contact coefficient but not the
geometric derivation on the right, forcing the constant to be one.

Result and local-field realization
Theorem 8.37 (Stress/Ward construction). The global displacement theorem plus the projective construc-
tion above produce a nonzero tempered Euclidean tensor insertion on the bounded geometric Schwinger
algebra, with exact translation contacts and signed-hypercubic covariance. The later angular Ward theorem
promotes its continuum multiplet to O(4) covariance.
8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance
Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification.
Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no
nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four,
modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives.
Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 by semisimplicity.
At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F ) and vanish. A dimension-four
gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving
one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded
by the stated quotient.


                                                         86
Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection-
even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or
five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six.
Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(Fµα Fνα ), δµν Tr F 2 , and
the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional
to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the
odd-derivative dimension-five sector.
Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric
regulator insertion is a2 times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds
therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4)
covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are
inserted.

Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional
renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration.
On J≤D , δω is a finite matrix, allowing same-dimension composite-operator mixing.
Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey

                                               [δω , δη ] = δ[ω,η]

on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2.
Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test
profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator
identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and
therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the
projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity.
   Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4).
The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π
rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4).
Theorem 8.45 (SO(4) covariance of the Schwinger family). Let ρD be the integrated finite-dimensional
action on J≤D . Then for every R ∈ SO(4),

                                  Sn (ρD (R)v1 , Rf1 ), . . . , (ρD (R)vn , Rfn )
                                                                                 

                                        = Sn (v1 , f1 ), . . . , (vn , fn ) .
                                                                           


Exact lattice reflection extends the covariance to O(4).
Proof. Along a smooth path R(s) in SO(4), differentiate the transformed Schwinger function. The
derivative is the left side of (165) with ω = R′ (s)R(s)−1 and therefore vanishes. Lemma 8.44 gives path
independence and the group law. Reflection is exact for the Wilson action, block map, clover source and
finite source normalization.



                                                       88
Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4)
covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the
chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time
with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there.
Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the
full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness
and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly
continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert
space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained
directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation
covariance.
Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source
multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The
direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses,
including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction
then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation
that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient.

9     Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap
9.1    Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap
The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time
translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed
by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional
S on D with the following properties:
(S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor;
(S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity;
(S3) continuous Euclidean translations;
(S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports,

                              |S(F G) − S(F )S(G)| ≤ CF,G e−m dist1 (supp F,supp G)                   (166)

     with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials;
(S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet.
The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not.
Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time
subalgebra. The form
                                        ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G)
is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1],
and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup

                                         T (t) = e−tH ,    H ≥ 0.

Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F ∥OS = 0}. Positive-time
translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define
contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core
vector [F ],
                                        2
                      ∥T (t)[F ] − [F ]∥ = S(ΘF τ2t F ) − 2ℜS(ΘF τt F ) + S(ΘF F ),
which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H.
Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T (t) = e−tH with
H ≥ 0.

Spectral consequence of one common clustering rate Let D+,0 be the centered positive-time
core,
                              D+,0 = {F ∈ D+ : S(F ) = 0}.
For F ∈ D+,0 let µF be the positive spectral measure of H in the vector [F ].


                                                     89
Lemma 9.2 (Core spectral support). For every F ∈ D+,0 ,

                                              supp µF ⊂ [m, ∞).                                       (167)

Proof. Let the time support of F lie in a fixed compact interval. For all sufficiently large t, the supports
of ΘF and τt F are separated by at least t − cF . Since S(F ) = 0, (166) and OS reconstruction give
                               Z ∞
                          0≤        e−tλ dµF (λ) = ⟨[F ], T (t)[F ]⟩ ≤ CF e−mt .                       (168)
                                  0

Fix ε > 0. Then
                                      e−(m−ε)t µF ([0, m − ε]) ≤ CF e−mt .
Multiplication by e(m−ε)t and t → ∞ gives µF ([0, m − ε]) = 0. Letting ε ↓ 0 proves (167).

Theorem 9.3 (Unique vacuum and complete local mass gap). The OS Hamiltonian satisfies

                                               H Ω⊥ ≥ m > 0.                                          (169)

The vacuum is unique.
Proof. The centered classes [F ] − S(F )Ω, F ∈ D+ , are dense in Ω⊥ . Lemma 9.2 says that the spectral
projection EH ([0, m − ε]) annihilates this dense set for every ε > 0; hence it vanishes on Ω⊥ . This proves
the absence of spectrum in (0, m).
   For a centered core vector, (168) tends to zero, so its spectral measure has no atom at zero. Density
then shows that EH ({0})H = CΩ. Thus the vacuum is unique and (169) holds.

10     Nontriviality and finite spectral window
10.1     Endpoint interpolation and reflected noncollapse
Let Yr (f ) be the tangent-normalized, Wick-centered scalar curvature source at physical scale r, with the
source root parallel transported in the normalized finite-jet frame. The test profile f is chosen once at
unit scale and dilated with the source so that its dimensionless support remains fixed and lies strictly in
positive Euclidean time.
Lemma 10.1 (Positive Gaussian OS norm). There is such a profile f for which

                                    V0 (f ) := S0 ΘYr (f ) Yr (f ) > 0.
                                                                  


The value is independent of r in the normalized frame.
Proof. At the Gaussian fixed point the centered scalar curvature composite creates a two-curvature Fock
vector. In Fourier variables its squared norm is an integral of the squared symmetrized two-particle
amplitude against the positive transverse curvature spectral density. That density is nonzero on an open
set, so choosing a positive-time profile whose Fourier transform is nonzero there gives a strictly positive
integral. Reflection positivity identifies the integral with the displayed OS form. Scale covariance of the
tangent-normalized source makes the value independent of r.

Theorem 10.4 (Endpoint packet-shell supplier). For the graph-segment family constructed above, the
complete twice-marked endpoint expansion has the following properties.
    (i) The grounded periodic covariance has volume-independent AA, AF , and F F rows of orders two,
        three, and four. After the exact global contact is subtracted, every complete reflection/BRS packet
        has two lattice-difference factors and therefore gains (r/ℓi )2 at weak scale i.
   (ii) The exact weak recurrence admits a finite crossing-cell decomposition. On each fixed cell, both g
        derivatives are taken before the labelled large-field partition and before bad-core ownership. The
        differentiated partition is then recollected with the same charged events and with no same-owner
        cancellation assumption.
 (iii) Before crossing, the full rooted stable map, including wrapping and charged-flat coordinates, is C 2
        in g with a strictly contracting same-order marked row. At first crossing, the fresh marked family
        lies in a uniform hybrid pivot–jet polydisc and satisfies the original-link Fourier–Wiener incidence
        row required by the joint Haar–optical collect.
  (iv) The crossing contribution and the no-crossing terminal contribution are controlled by one geometri-

        its first two g derivatives, extends to the closed crossing cells, and sends the terminal remainder to
        zero uniformly as the terminal depth tends to infinity.

root, has a cutoff-, volume-, crossing-cell-, and terminal-depth-independent C 2 bound on 0 ≤ g ≤ gr .
Proof. Appendix A gives the full proof inside this manuscript. Its five subsections establish the grounded-

Proposition 8.36 (Noncollapse and canonical normalization). The stress Schwinger distribution is
nonzero. Its normalization is fixed uniquely by the unit contact coefficient in (160); no independent finite
factor is available.
Proof. The reflected curvature source of Theorem 10.5 (proved independently in Section 10) defines

distributional translation derivative is nonzero for suitable separated test profiles. Choose v supported
near one insertion. The right side of (160) is then nonzero, so the tensor insertion on the left cannot
vanish. Multiplying the tensor by a finite constant would multiply the left contact coefficient but not the
geometric derivation on the right, forcing the constant to be one.

algebra, with exact translation contacts and signed-hypercubic covariance. The later angular Ward theorem
promotes its continuum multiplet to O(4) covariance.
8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance
Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification.
Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no
nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four,
modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives.
Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 by semisimplicity.
At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F ) and vanish. A dimension-four
gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving
one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded
by the stated quotient.


                                                         86
Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection-
even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or
five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six.
Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(Fµα Fνα ), δµν Tr F 2 , and
the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional
to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the
odd-derivative dimension-five sector.
Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric
regulator insertion is a2 times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds
therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4)
covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are
inserted.

Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional
renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration.
On J≤D , δω is a finite matrix, allowing same-dimension composite-operator mixing.
Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey

                                               [δω , δη ] = δ[ω,η]

on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2.
Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test
profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator
identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and
therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the
projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity.
   Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4).
The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π
rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4).
Theorem 8.45 (SO(4) covariance of the Schwinger family). Let ρD be the integrated finite-dimensional
action on J≤D . Then for every R ∈ SO(4),

                                  Sn (ρD (R)v1 , Rf1 ), . . . , (ρD (R)vn , Rfn )

                                        = Sn (v1 , f1 ), . . . , (vn , fn ) .

Exact lattice reflection extends the covariance to O(4).
Proof. Along a smooth path R(s) in SO(4), differentiate the transformed Schwinger function. The
derivative is the left side of (165) with ω = R′ (s)R(s)−1 and therefore vanishes. Lemma 8.44 gives path
independence and the group law. Reflection is exact for the Wilson action, block map, clover source and
finite source normalization.



                                                       88
Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4)
covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the
chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time
with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there.
Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the
full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness
and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly
continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert
space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained
directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation
covariance.
Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source
multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The
direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses,
including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction
then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation
that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient.

9     Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap
9.1    Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap
The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time
translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed
by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional
S on D with the following properties:
(S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor;
(S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity;
(S3) continuous Euclidean translations;
(S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports,

                              |S(F G) − S(F )S(G)| ≤ CF,G e−m dist1 (supp F,supp G)                   (166)

     with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials;
(S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet.
The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not.
Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time
subalgebra. The form
                                        ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G)
is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1],
and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup

                                         T (t) = e−tH ,    H ≥ 0.

Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F ∥OS = 0}. Positive-time
translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define
contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core
vector [F ],
                                        2
                      ∥T (t)[F ] − [F ]∥ = S(ΘF τ2t F ) − 2ℜS(ΘF τt F ) + S(ΘF F ),
which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H.
Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T (t) = e−tH with
H ≥ 0.

Spectral consequence of one common clustering rate Let D+,0 be the centered positive-time
core,
                              D+,0 = {F ∈ D+ : S(F ) = 0}.
For F ∈ D+,0 let µF be the positive spectral measure of H in the vector [F ].

Lemma 9.2 (Core spectral support). For every F ∈ D+,0 ,

                                              supp µF ⊂ [m, ∞).                                       (167)

Proof. Let the time support of F lie in a fixed compact interval. For all sufficiently large t, the supports
of ΘF and τt F are separated by at least t − cF . Since S(F ) = 0, (166) and OS reconstruction give
                               Z ∞

                                  0

Fix ε > 0. Then
                                      e−(m−ε)t µF ([0, m − ε]) ≤ CF e−mt .
Multiplication by e(m−ε)t and t → ∞ gives µF ([0, m − ε]) = 0. Letting ε ↓ 0 proves (167).

Theorem 9.3 (Unique vacuum and complete local mass gap). The OS Hamiltonian satisfies

                                               H Ω⊥ ≥ m > 0.                                          (169)

The vacuum is unique.
Proof. The centered classes [F ] − S(F )Ω, F ∈ D+ , are dense in Ω⊥ . Lemma 9.2 says that the spectral
projection EH ([0, m − ε]) annihilates this dense set for every ε > 0; hence it vanishes on Ω⊥ . This proves
the absence of spectrum in (0, m).
   For a centered core vector, (168) tends to zero, so its spectral measure has no atom at zero. Density
then shows that EH ({0})H = CΩ. Thus the vacuum is unique and (169) holds.

10     Nontriviality and finite spectral window
10.1     Endpoint interpolation and reflected noncollapse
Let Yr (f ) be the tangent-normalized, Wick-centered scalar curvature source at physical scale r, with the
source root parallel transported in the normalized finite-jet frame. The test profile f is chosen once at
unit scale and dilated with the source so that its dimensionless support remains fixed and lies strictly in
positive Euclidean time.
Lemma 10.1 (Positive Gaussian OS norm). There is such a profile f for which

                                    V0 (f ) := S0 ΘYr (f ) Yr (f ) > 0.
                                                                  


The value is independent of r in the normalized frame.
Proof. At the Gaussian fixed point the centered scalar curvature composite creates a two-curvature Fock
vector. In Fourier variables its squared norm is an integral of the squared symmetrized two-particle
amplitude against the positive transverse curvature spectral density. That density is nonzero on an open
set, so choosing a positive-time profile whose Fourier transform is nonzero there gives a strictly positive
integral. Reflection positivity identifies the integral with the displayed OS form. Scale covariance of the
tangent-normalized source makes the value independent of r.

Theorem 10.4 (Endpoint packet-shell supplier). For the graph-segment family constructed above, the
complete twice-marked endpoint expansion has the following properties.
    (i) The grounded periodic covariance has volume-independent AA, AF , and F F rows of orders two,
        three, and four. After the exact global contact is subtracted, every complete reflection/BRS packet
        has two lattice-difference factors and therefore gains (r/ℓi )2 at weak scale i.
   (ii) The exact weak recurrence admits a finite crossing-cell decomposition. On each fixed cell, both g
        derivatives are taken before the labelled large-field partition and before bad-core ownership. The
        differentiated partition is then recollected with the same charged events and with no same-owner
        cancellation assumption.
 (iii) Before crossing, the full rooted stable map, including wrapping and charged-flat coordinates, is C 2
        in g with a strictly contracting same-order marked row. At first crossing, the fresh marked family
        lies in a uniform hybrid pivot–jet polydisc and satisfies the original-link Fourier–Wiener incidence
        row required by the joint Haar–optical collect.
  (iv) The crossing contribution and the no-crossing terminal contribution are controlled by one geometri-
        cally summable conditional-packet shell majorant. The same majorant controls the functional and
        its first two g derivatives, extends to the closed crossing cells, and sends the terminal remainder to
        zero uniformly as the terminal depth tends to infinity.

root, has a cutoff-, volume-, crossing-cell-, and terminal-depth-independent C 2 bound on 0 ≤ g ≤ gr .
Proof. Appendix A gives the full proof inside this manuscript. Its five subsections establish the grounded-

entrance estimates; and the terminal-shell summation. No additional mathematical input is imported

11     Completion for arbitrary fixed compact simple gauge groups
Theorem 11.1 (Completion of the fixed-group construction). For every fixed compact connected simple
G and faithful unitary representation ρ, the group register CG is finite and all hypotheses consumed by
Sections 2–10 are satisfied. Consequently Theorem 1.1 holds.
Proof. Compactness and the analytic bi-invariant geometry give the Karcher, logarithm, coarea, and
faithful-action constants of Section 2. The complete folded block symbol is spacetime-only and tensors
with Ig , so the strict right-inverse and optical coercivity constants are unchanged. The chart complement
is charged by the faithful Wilson action and the irregular activity is exponentially small. The permanent
weak map, centered graph, and BPHZL packet use the same finite-dimensional Lie-algebra tensor calculus,
with the universal color factor CA (G).
   The Peter–Weyl spectral sum converges at the fixed order sG , and the higher hybrid-polydisc argument
supplies the one-pivot nuclear and multipivot Fourier–Wiener rows. The ordinary-periodic toron, seam,
reflection, and wrapping templates are admitted by Theorem D.3; no twisted sector or finite-volume bundle
sum is used. The invariant-jet theorem supplies the complete low-dimensional mixing and dimension-six
regulator spaces. The joint Haar–o

Proof. Integrate (160) by parts in v and use the arbitrariness of the compactly supported profile. Locality
separates the ordinary contact terms from pairwise-disjoint supports.
Proposition 8.36 (Noncollapse and canonical normalization). The stress Schwinger distribution is
nonzero. Its normalization is fixed uniquely by the unit contact coefficient in (160); no independent finite
factor is available.
Proof. The reflected curvature source of Theorem 10.5 (proved independently in Section 10) defines
a nonzero centered local vector. Common physical clustering gives its time-translation correlation a
finite positive spectral measure supported away from zero; hence that correlation is not constant and its
distributional translation derivative is nonzero for suitable separated test profiles. Choose v supported
near one insertion. The right side of (160) is then nonzero, so the tensor insertion on the left cannot
vanish. Multiplying the tensor by a finite constant would multiply the left contact coefficient but not the
geometric derivation on the right, forcing the constant to be one.

Result and local-field realization
Theorem 8.37 (Stress/Ward construction). The global displacement theorem plus the projective construc-
tion above produce a nonzero tempered Euclidean tensor insertion on the bounded geometric Schwinger
algebra, with exact translation contacts and signed-hypercubic covariance. The later angular Ward theorem
promotes its continuum multiplet to O(4) covariance.
8.2.5 Euclidean rotation covariance
Low-dimensional rotation-defect classification.
Lemma 8.38 (No low-dimensional gauge-invariant vector). For every compact simple G there is no
nonzero local, reflection-compatible, gauge-invariant vector source of engineering dimension at most four,
modulo vacuum terms and total derivatives.
Proof. A connection mass term is not gauge invariant. At dimension two, Tr dρ(Fµν ) = 0 by semisimplicity.
At dimension three, traces of dρ(DF ) reduce to derivatives of Tr dρ(F ) and vanish. A dimension-four
gauge-invariant vector cannot be formed from two curvature two-forms using only the metric while leaving
one free index; an epsilon tensor would have the wrong reflection parity. Total derivatives are excluded
by the stated quotient.


                                                         86
Lemma 8.39 (Antisymmetric stress defect begins at dimension six). A local signed-hypercubic, reflection-
even, gauge-invariant antisymmetric rank-two source has no component of engineering dimension four or
five. Its first possible nonzero continuum jet has dimension at least six.
Proof. At dimension four the quadratic curvature tensors are generated by Tr(Fµα Fνα ), δµν Tr F 2 , and
the corresponding dual-curvature contractions. The first is symmetric; the dual contraction is proportional
to a diagonal pseudoscalar tensor; none has an antisymmetric part. Reflection symmetry excludes the
odd-derivative dimension-five sector.
Corollary 8.40 (Vanishing rotation regulator). In the projective stress Ward identity, the antisymmetric
regulator insertion is a2 times a normalized family of dimension-six roots. The marked source bounds
therefore make it vanish in the zero-spacing limit. The cutoff rotation Ward identities integrate to full O(4)
covariance once the already established translation, reflection, and tensor-source continuity statements are
inserted.

Finite source multiplets and integration For every D < ∞, let J≤D be the finite-dimensional
renormalized curvature-jet source multiplet. The contact hierarchy preserves the engineering filtration.
On J≤D , δω is a finite matrix, allowing same-dimension composite-operator mixing.
Lemma 8.44 (Rotation algebra on finite source multiplets). The contact derivations obey

                                               [δω , δη ] = δ[ω,η]

on every J≤D and are compatible under D 7→ D + 2.
Proof. At finite cutoff the contact derivations are obtained by differentiating the geometric pullback of test
profiles, tensor indices and local source words. Geometric Lie derivatives obey the displayed commutator
identity exactly. Finite triangular source renormalization conjugates this finite-dimensional action and
therefore preserves the commutator. Dimension-raised regulator contacts vanish by Lemma 8.41; the
projective limit and the inclusions of source multiplets preserve the identity.
   Every finite-dimensional real representation of so(4) integrates to the simply connected group Spin(4).
The source multiplets consist of bosonic tensors built from curvature and covariant derivatives, so a 2π
rotation acts trivially; equivalently, their geometric tensor action factors through SO(4).
Theorem 8.45 (SO(4) covariance of the Schwinger family). Let ρD be the integrated finite-dimensional
action on J≤D . Then for every R ∈ SO(4),

Exact lattice reflection extends the covariance to O(4).
Proof. Along a smooth path R(s) in SO(4), differentiate the transformed Schwinger function. The
derivative is the left side of (165) with ω = R′ (s)R(s)−1 and therefore vanishes. Lemma 8.44 gives path

Osterwalder–Schrader continuation Let Dloc be the local field-polynomial test algebra. The O(4)
covariance proved above is an identity of the Euclidean Schwinger system. Rotations preserving the
chosen time-zero hyperplane act directly on the positive-time quotient; rotations mixing Euclidean time
with space need not preserve that quotient and are not asserted to act unitarily there.
Theorem 8.46 (Euclidean covariance and Poincaré reconstruction). The Schwinger family satisfies the
full O(4) covariance axiom. Together with reflection positivity, permutation symmetry, temperedness
and continuous translations, this gives by the Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction theorem a strongly
continuous unitary representation of the proper orthochronous Poincaré group on the reconstructed Hilbert
space, with translation spectrum in the closed forward cone. Spatial rotations and translations are obtained
directly on the OS quotient; Lorentz boosts arise from analytic continuation of the Euclidean rotation
covariance.
Proof. Theorem 8.45 is precisely the Euclidean covariance identity required for every finite tensor/source
multiplet, and the nested source construction extends it to the complete chosen local field family. The
direct OS theorem, Lemma 8.14, and Lemma 8.13 supply the remaining reconstruction hypotheses,
including the linear-growth condition. The standard Osterwalder–Schrader covariance reconstruction
then produces the positive- energy Poincaré representation. No unitary action of a Euclidean rotation
that changes the reflection hyperplane is assumed on the intermediate OS quotient.

9     Continuum reconstruction and spectral gap
9.1    Osterwalder–Schrader reconstruction and clustering gap
The common Schwinger functional Let D be the countable algebra generated by positive-time
translates of the bounded positive-scale cylinders and the normalized finite local source fields constructed
by the marked hierarchy. The multiscale source-tree theorem supplies a projective Schwinger functional
S on D with the following properties:
(S1) temperedness and continuity in every Schwartz test tensor;
(S2) stationarity, gauge invariance, permutation symmetry, and reflection positivity;
(S3) continuous Euclidean translations;
(S4) for every two field polynomials F, G with separated supports,

                              |S(F G) − S(F )S(G)| ≤ CF,G e−m dist1 (supp F,supp G)                   (166)

     with one m > 0 independent of the chosen polynomials;
(S5) the OS linear-growth estimate of Lemma 8.13 on every finite source multiplet.
The constants CF,G may depend on the fixed fields and test functions; the exponent m does not.
Theorem 9.1 (Continuous OS reconstruction). Let Θ be time reflection and D+ the positive-time
subalgebra. The form
                                        ⟨F, G⟩OS = S(ΘF G)
is positive semidefinite. Its quotient and completion give a separable Hilbert space H, vacuum Ω = [1],
and a strongly continuous self-adjoint contraction semigroup

                                         T (t) = e−tH ,    H ≥ 0.

Proof. Reflection positivity gives the positive form and the null ideal N = {F : ∥F ∥OS = 0}. Positive-time
translations preserve D+ for sufficiently small translations on each compactly supported word and define
contractions on the quotient; the usual time-zero limiting argument extends them to all t ≥ 0. For a core
vector [F ],
                                        2
                      ∥T (t)[F ] − [F ]∥ = S(ΘF τ2t F ) − 2ℜS(ΘF τt F ) + S(ΘF F ),
which tends to zero by translation continuity. Contractivity and density give strong continuity on H.
Reflection symmetry makes the semigroup self-adjoint, and the spectral theorem gives T (t) = e−tH with
H ≥ 0.

Spectral consequence of one common clustering rate Let D+,0 be the centered positive-time
core,
                              D+,0 = {F ∈ D+ : S(F ) = 0}.
For F ∈ D+,0 let µF be the positive spectral measure of H in the vector [F ].

Lemma 9.2 (Core spectral support). For every F ∈ D+,0 ,

                                              supp µF ⊂ [m, ∞).                                       (167)

Proof. Let the time support of F lie in a fixed compact interval. For all sufficiently large t, the supports

                               Z ∞

Fix ε > 0. Then
                                      e−(m−ε)t µF ([0, m − ε]) ≤ CF e−mt .
Multiplication by e(m−ε)t and t → ∞ gives µF ([0, m − ε]) = 0. Letting ε ↓ 0 proves (167).

Theorem 9.3 (Unique vacuum and complete local mass gap). The OS Hamiltonian satisfies

                                               H Ω⊥ ≥ m > 0.                                          (169)

The vacuum is unique.
Proof. The centered classes [F ] − S(F )Ω, F ∈ D+ , are dense in Ω⊥ . Lemma 9.2 says that the spectral
projection EH ([0, m − ε]) annihilates this dense set for every ε > 0; hence it vanishes on Ω⊥ . This proves
the absence of spectrum in (0, m).
   For a centered core vector, (168) tends to zero, so its spectral measure has no atom at zero. Density
then shows that EH ({0})H = CΩ. Thus the vacuum is unique and (169) holds.

10     Nontriviality and finite spectral window
10.1     Endpoint interpolation and reflected noncollapse
Let Yr (f ) be the tangent-normalized, Wick-centered scalar curvature source at physical scale r, with the
source root parallel transported in the normalized finite-jet frame. The test profile f is chosen once at
unit scale and dilated with the source so that its dimensionless support remains fixed and lies strictly in
positive Euclidean time.
Lemma 10.1 (Positive Gaussian OS norm). There is such a profile f for which

                                    V0 (f ) := S0 ΘYr (f ) Yr (f ) > 0.
                                                                  


The value is independent of r in the normalized frame.
Proof. At the Gaussian fixed point the centered scalar curvature composite creates a two-curvature Fock
vector. In Fourier variables its squared norm is an integral of the squared symmetrized two-particle
amplitude against the positive transverse curvature spectral density. That density is nonzero on an open
set, so choosing a positive-time profile whose Fourier transform is nonzero there gives a strictly positive
integral. Reflection positivity identifies the integral with the displayed OS form. Scale covariance of the
tangent-normalized source makes the value independent of r.

Theorem 10.4 (Endpoint packet-shell supplier). For the graph-segment family constructed above, the
complete twice-marked endpoint expansion has the following properties.
    (i) The grounded periodic covariance has volume-independent AA, AF , and F F rows of orders two,
        three, and four. After the exact global contact is subtracted, every complete reflection/BRS packet
        has two lattice-difference factors and therefore gains (r/ℓi )2 at weak scale i.
   (ii) The exact weak recurrence admits a finite crossing-cell decomposition. On each fixed cell, both g
        derivatives are taken before the labelled large-field partition and before bad-core ownership. The
        differentiated partition is then recollected with the same charged events and with no same-owner
        cancellation assumption.
 (iii) Before crossing, the full rooted stable map, including wrapping and charged-flat coordinates, is C 2
        in g with a strictly contracting same-order marked row. At first crossing, the fresh marked family
        lies in a uniform hybrid pivot–jet polydisc and satisfies the original-link Fourier–Wiener incidence
        row required by the joint Haar–optical collect.
  (iv) The crossing contribution and the no-crossing terminal contribution are controlled by one geometri-
        cally summable conditional-packet shell majorant. The same majorant controls the functional and
        its first two g derivatives, extends to the closed crossing cells, and sends the terminal remainder to
        zero uniformly as the terminal depth tends to infinity.
Consequently the complete normalized two-root functional, with or without one fixed dimension-six defect
root, has a cutoff-, volume-, crossing-cell-, and terminal-depth-independent C 2 bound on 0 ≤ g ≤ gr .
Proof. Appendix A gives the full proof inside this manuscript. Its five subsections establish the grounded-
torus covariance and globally subtracted packet division; the weak crossing recurrence and wrapping rows;
the twice-marked conditional-packet contraction; the pre-owner hybrid-polydisc Wiener and compact-
entrance estimates; and the terminal-shell summation. No additional mathematical input is imported
beyond the results proved in Sections 3–7.

Compatibility of the physical scale with the common diagonal. Let f be the profile of
Lemma 10.1, and let the constants Cf , pf be those in the uniform endpoint and source-defect rows
above. Choose the fixed countable source-scale set used in Corollary 8.12 to contain a cofinal dyadic
sequence rn ↓ 0. There is an index n such that, for r = rn ,
                                                         1
                                     Cf u(r) + r2 L(r)pf ≤ V0 (f ).
                                       
                                                          2
Before taking the common diagonal subsequence, enlarge the countable source algebra by the fixed fields
Yr (f ), ΘYr (f ), and their reflected product. The same single diagonal extraction then gives all Schwinger
limits entering their OS form and preserves every cutoff- and volume-uniform lower bound. The scale r is
not changed after extraction. The cofinal dyadic choice is countable, while u(r) → 0 and r2 L(r)pf → 0
along it. Hence one r satisfies the displayed inequality. Adding finitely many fields at that scale preserves
countability of the source algebra. The source-tree theorem and Corollary 8.12 therefore permit the
same diagonal argument, and convergence of the finitely many Schwinger functions passes a uniform
finite-cutoff lower bound to the limit.

Corollary 10.7 (Finite positive local spectral window without Wilson pushforward). Let ψY be the
nonzero limiting curvature vector from Lemma 10.6. If common physical clustering and the OS spectral
argument give
                                    supp µY ⊂ [m⋆ , ∞),  m⋆ > 0,

                                           µY ([m⋆ , MY ]) > 0.

MY = n.

11     Completion for arbitrary fixed compact simple gauge groups
Theorem 11.1 (Completion of the fixed-group construction). For every fixed compact connected simple
G and faithful unitary representation ρ, the group register CG is finite and all hypotheses consumed by
Sections 2–10 are satisfied. Consequently Theorem 1.1 holds.
Proof. Compactness and the analytic bi-invariant geometry give the Karcher, logarithm, coarea, and
faithful-action constants of Section 2. The complete folded block symbol is spacetime-only and tensors
with Ig , so the strict right-inverse and optical coercivity constants are unchanged. The chart complement
is charged by the faithful Wilson action and the irregular activity is exponentially small. The permanent
weak map, centered graph, and BPHZL packet use the same finite-dimensional Lie-algebra tensor calculus,
with the universal color factor CA (G).
   The Peter–Weyl spectral sum converges at the fixed order sG , and the higher hybrid-polydisc argument
supplies the one-pivot nuclear and multipivot Fourier–Wiener rows. The ordinary-periodic toron, seam,
reflection, and wrapping templates are admitted by Theorem D.3; no twisted sector or finite-volume bundle
sum is used. The invariant-jet theorem supplies the complete low-dimensional mixing and dimension-six
regulator spaces. The joint Haar–optical forest, source transport, Ward hierarchy, OS reconstruction,
and noncollapse theorem then apply with constants depending on G but uniform in cutoff, volume, and
directed depth. Appendix F, specifically Table 3, records every independent entry of CG , the first proof
call at which its G-dependence enters, and the subsequent consumers. Thus no additional estimate
uniform in G is hidden in this completion step: after those finite calls, each remaining mechanism is
color-free, tensors with Ig , or takes a finite maximum in a registered finite-dimensional space.
Corollary 11.2 (The SU(N ) family). For every fixed integer N ≥ 2, the theorem holds for G = SU(N )
in the defining representation, and the scale-two map is
